This text applies framing A as document skeleton, panelist 1 authority/schema/receipt, panelist 3 C1–C6, and the round-1 critique's freeze bar. It does **not** fold B's host-spawn route or ref-only journal. The owner's Q1 answer adopts LockScope option (a) of §5, a Rust-owned scope that receives the caller's held ranks and lease descriptors as explicit, kernel-verifiable parameters; this is B's `heldRanks` made kernel-verifiable, never a bitmap. The reachability pin is installed and read back in prepare, **before** the single-ref target CAS; a multi-ref `update-ref --stdin` transaction is never treated as crash-atomic (the Git floor proof killed one between ref installs, §4, §10). The file WAL remains the only receipt authority.

## 1. Purpose and boundary

Git decides what Git state exists, performs typed authorized mutations, and reconciles their interrupted effects. Rust is the live core now; Node transports typed inputs/results and owns no Git policy. There is no public `git(args)` port and no dual-run writer. Workflow, Execution Core, Work, business workflows, setup and Event Store are consumers, not parallel Git cores.

Git owns local object/ref/index/worktree mutations, worktree registration, the local side of fetch/push, and operation recovery. It owns neither business approval nor Work lifecycle, claim/state transaction, reducer, event envelope/union, seal schema, coverage or consumer cache. Holder-lock owns locks/fences; Process owns containment/signals; Session Identity owns writer identity. A read-only request never silently commits, builds, creates a worktree, configures a remote or repairs a repo.

**Projects without Git.** A read-only Unit needs no RepoHandle. When the directory is not a repository, Inspect returns the typed result `notARepository` and Git never turns the absence of a repository into a workflow error; whether a consumer (for example a business workflow, mission #2) proceeds without one is the consumer's decision. A mutation still requires a validated repository.

Required component-boundary row on acceptance: **Git — foundation; owns repository facts, typed Git operations and recovery WAL; consumers Workflow/Execution/Work/Event Store/setup; dependencies holder-lock/process/session-identity; no consumer imports.** Remove “pure git integration” from Workflow's ownership. The signing bundle (2026-10-18) installed this row in `docs/platform/component-boundary.md` and removed “pure git integration” from the Workflow row.

| Mutation layer | Owner / success boundary |
|---|---|
| Local objects/refs | Git; expected-old publication says nothing about checkout or network |
| Local index/worktree/admin | Git; scoped commit/sync/land/restore/create/remove/relocate |
| Remote send/receive | Git transport port; explicit ref mapping and separate remote acknowledgement |
| GitHub API merge | GitHub adapter under consumer authorization; never a local land and never a policy bypass |

A network failure cannot undo local success. API timeout is outcome-unknown until queried; do not blindly issue another merge. GitHub transport must retain consumer approval/Iron Law and record the actual merge commit. This spec authorizes no external send by itself.

Git never imports a consumer. Verify/build/npm/`worktreeSetup.commands` are **not** Git operations (G-030/G-031). Execution provisions after `worktree-create` returns, **outside every Git lock**: the `fgos worktree create` verb runs the dependency install and `worktreeSetup.commands` from its use case, after the Git port has returned. Git may spawn only `git` (plus Process containment of those children). Consumer verification evidence is bound by digest; Git does not claim tests ran.

## 2. Shared entities and outcomes

| Entity | Contract |
|---|---|
| RepoHandle | Validated canonical common-dir and trusted main/root identity/topology; never worker `.git` authority |
| WorktreeHandle | Registered gitdir/backlink, canonical path, branch/detached OID, creation identity; revalidated at mutation |
| Oid | Full object-format-aware identifier; validated commit/tree/blob type, no fixed SHA-1 assumption |
| RefName / ExpectedTip | Full validated namespace-qualified **direct** ref; expected `Absent` or exact OID, never wildcard absence. Symbolic refs are refused in owned and mirror namespaces (C1). |
| Scope | Canonical RUL84 entries, matcher version and explicitly expanded generated grants |
| Custody token | The existing `dispatch-cwd` lease (rank 30) on the worktree, held by Execution and extended across the runner commit (owner decision Q2, section 6). Git **validates** the lease descriptor by kernel check and fails closed when it is missing or stale; it does not mint a second lock and there is no Git `Custody` entity. A text session/writer id is not custody. |
| Candidate | Immutable source/target OIDs, exact result tree, parent/mode, **policy evidence digest**, pin |
| OperationId | Repo-scoped idempotency key bound to kind, repo identity, **canonical resource set**, expected values and authority domain; same id with different request digest refused; same id must not bind two disjoint resource sets |
| Receipt | Structured effect receipt (below). Operational evidence, not a consumer event |

Receipt (panelist 1 / C3) — effect is not completion:

```text
operationId, requestDigest,
localEffect {expected, publishedOid?, observedTip, publicationState},
checkout {notApplicable | pending | reconciled | recoveryRequired},
durability {processCrash | stableMedia, capabilityEvidence},
remote {notRequested | acknowledged | unknown | rejected},
consumerSettlement {notOwnedByGit},
attribution {thisInvocationWonCas | effectObservedOnly | uncertain},
warnings?   (facts that did not fail the operation, such as a compaction left for a later call)
```

`publicationState` distinguishes “currently at candidate” from “this invocation's CAS was observed successful.” Seeing `ref == next` after a crash proves the desired state exists; it does **not** prove which actor installed it. Do not manufacture writer attribution from equality. ABA (target returned to expected after a possible successful CAS) does not justify generic retry: a target CAS whose outcome is unknown is never reissued (§10 TargetAttempt rule). The CAS winner is defined per attempt recorded in the WAL, not by OID value over history: exact-old CAS gives at most one successful state-changing CAS per resource within one uninterrupted expected-state generation, and two CASes from the same expected OID across an ABA are both correct Git semantics. OID equality never identifies a generation; per-operation at-most-once is a WAL property, not a CAS property.

Results distinguish the result codes `unchanged`, `committed`, `busy`, `stale`, `refused`, `failed`, `committed-but-contract-violation`, `remote-outcome-unknown`, `recovery-required` and `superseded` (`result-codes.v1.json`; `superseded` is terminal, PublicationCas only, §10 rule 4a: a record-only outcome that says nothing about whether the old CAS ran). Sync states (`conflicted-open`, `conflict-aborted`) are not result codes. Two outcome kinds are not result codes either, and their stable machine names are not new codes: `abandonedBeforeAttempt` (terminal, pre-attempt PublicationCas, ScopedCommit, Sync or Land, §10 rule 4b: no TargetAttempt, so no target effect) is reported as the cause `abandoned-before-attempt` under `refused`, and `configApproved` (terminal, ConfigChange only, §10 rule 4c: an effect-free re-approval of existing fetch mappings) is reported as a successful config receipt. Include operation id, typed cause, retryability, actual effect/OIDs, exit/signal and bounded redacted stderr. An ancestry query returns yes/no/error; path-at-revision present/absent/error. Corruption, permission and unknown protocol never become “absent”. An ancestry walk that crosses a shallow boundary returns `shallow-history-incomplete` (an error, never “no”), even when both endpoint objects are present: the Git floor proof saw `merge-base --is-ancestor` exit 1 for a true parent after the parent was fetched into a shallow clone. Endpoint presence is not traversal completeness, and cleanup (§11) never treats such an answer as delivery proof. Cancellation after CAS reports the published effect. Local commit, reconciled checkout, remote ack and durable consumer settlement are separate facts.

## 3. Typed operations

All mutations require operation id, validated repository, writer and **declared resource set + existing authority**. No option-bag escape to raw Git args. Reads are bounded/paginated and do not repair. Doctor never mutates.

| Port | Operations and guarantees |
|---|---|
| Inspect | resolveRepo/Ref, status (optionally leaving a submodule's own dirt out, or also listing ignored paths), diff (two commits or trees, or a commit or the index against the checkout's index or work tree; optional literal path prefix and rename detection), diffText (review patch over the same sides, optional literal paths and context lines, explicit output bound up to 50 MiB, no external diff/textconv), ancestry, readObjects, pathsAtRevision (batched), listWorktrees, compareContent, snapshotWrites, lastPathCommitTime (bounded literal paths), mergeTree, listTree, attributes, firstAncestryMerge, commitTime, countCommits, unmatchedCommitCount, trackedPaths, remoteUrl; explicit unknown/error and byte-safe paths; every Inspect call runs with optional locks off (`--no-optional-locks`), because `status` otherwise refreshes the index under `index.lock` |
| Refs | ensureBranch, advanceRef, deleteRef, fastForwardCheckedOut; exact-old CAS, never blind `branch -D`. Exact-old CAS does **not** protect a checked-out branch (`update-ref -d` deleted the current branch in the Git floor proof, and the V-CAS re-check deleted a branch that a worktree registered after the snapshot had checked out): every Refs mutation of `refs/heads/*` takes worktree-admin 40 (§5), and under that one hold revalidates every registered checkout (main and linked; a symbolic HEAD to an unborn name counts as checked out), performs the exact-old CAS, reads back the ref and the registered checkouts, and only then releases 40, so no fgOS worktree registration can come between the check and the effect. An earlier snapshot of checkout membership protects nothing. An in-use ref is refused. No typed operation switches the branch of an existing checkout; one added later takes 40 first. A switch by raw Git or a worker is outside the concurrency guarantee (§9) and is reported as `recovery-required` when that read-back sees it. The one exception is `fastForwardCheckedOut(branch, expectedOld, next)`: fast-forward only, exact-old CAS, the branch is checked out only in a worktree whose verified `dispatch-cwd` lease the caller holds, that checkout is clean, and its tree is updated under that custody; anything else is refused |
| Worktree | create, createDetached, relocate, remove, inspectOrphans, repairRegistration; one admin door, no rm-rf fallback. Git returns after registration/strip of worker `.fgos`; provisioning is Execution. |
| ScopedCommit | preflight/commit; foreign index refusal, custody, literal exact selection, postcommit set/tree proof. A tracked file move is a ScopedCommit with both literal paths (old deleted, new added); failure restores both paths to their immediately pre-commit existence, mode and content before any consumer registry update (a producer move already made before commit remains old-absent/new-present); there is no separate file-move operation |
| Sync | start/inspect/continue/abort; pinned target, scoped resolver, operation-owned merge state |
| Land | prepare/publish/recover; immutable candidate, merge or squash, journalled CAS/checkout. Consumer supplies verification evidence. |
| SafeRestore | preview/apply/recover/listSnapshot/extractPath/acknowledge; full locked checks and durable snapshot before destructive effect. `apply` may carry `reapplyLocalDelta`: after the snapshot and the restore to the new tip, the captured local delta is applied to index and tree; a failed reapply leaves the durable snapshot and returns `recovery-required` (replaces `resyncWorktree`'s `reset --hard` + `apply --index`) |
| Checkpoint adapter | checkpointTrackedFiles; detachable, consumer-selected **non-event** tracked payload only |
| RepositoryConfig | publicationFetchMappings(remote), ensureFetchMapping, ensureHooks, removeOwnedHooksConfig; local config, user settings preserved, provenance |
| Remote | pushRefs (the Node arm; `fetch_refs` and `inspect_remote` stay in the Rust driver with no Node arm); explicit mapping, no implicit all-refs or default push-config mutation |
| Recovery | inspectOperation, listPending, reconcile; actual disk/ref checks and idempotent results |
| Publication | restricted plumbing; section 4. Event Store is the only production writer of event refs |

**HEAD observed by a listing.** `resolveHead` reads HEAD with two bracketed children (`symbolic-ref`, then one `rev-parse` that must name the same target, else `head-changed-during-read`), because one `rev-parse` reads HEAD several times and could pair one branch's commit with another's name. A ref listing that includes `refs/heads/` among its prefixes returns, as `RefPage.head`, the branch HEAD named and that branch's own listed value, taken from the `%(HEAD)` mark of the same `for-each-ref` child: that child reads HEAD once at its start and reads the branch row during iteration, so the pair is always coherent (the commit is the named branch's own value, never another branch's) with the same freshness as `resolveHead`. Detached HEAD, unborn HEAD, a HEAD on a symbolic or non-`refs/heads/` ref, and a branch not among the listed prefixes mark nothing (`head` is `None`) and the caller keeps `resolveHead`. Across paginated pages a HEAD marked differently on two pages is no observation. The Event Store's tip listing includes `refs/heads/`, so an attached HEAD costs it no separate HEAD children; it lists at most 1024 rows per child and stops at the first page that reaches the branches (the event refs sort before them and are complete by then), so a HEAD branch past that page is unobserved and the read keeps `resolveHead`; it uses the listed fact only when the listed repository is the authority checkout itself.

Phase 07 SafeRestore extends its existing request with `checkoutBaseline` (optional per-worktree reflog OID) and `discardLocalDelta` (explicit retry cleanup), `requireCleanBaseline` (automatic claim reattach) and `requireStagedOnly` (manual staged repair). Clean/staged-only requirements are checked from the captured baseline delta under the same custody hold as the reset, never only in a Node pre-check. The former binds the actual index/files baseline when a branch advanced externally: it must equal this checkout's latest HEAD reflog and be an ancestor of the target. The discard mode is producer-only and pins its previous HEAD through the existing operation-pin door until snapshot acknowledgement; it permits a snapshot-backed reset to a recorded ancestor and removes only captured untracked paths after the durable snapshot; it cannot combine with reapply. Linked checkouts may contain the Worktree port's stripped tracked `.fgos` entries: only those skip-worktree flags are admitted; platform state is excluded from snapshots/deltas and re-stripped after reset under custody. Main state remains refused. SafeRestore owns the existing `fgos-resync-patches/<branch>-<operation>.patch` binary artifact: it installs before reset, preserves on conflict/interruption, removes unchanged bytes only after a durable success receipt, and refuses branch-matching orphan patches under custody. Inspect returns those paths for the consumer no-op guard. Recovery uses the same request and durable snapshot; no caller reimplements patch/reset/clean.

Phase 07 Worktree callers use additional bounded Inspect facts: `compareCommits` returns merge-base, left/right counts and the exact right-only OIDs; `listBranches` returns literal local branch refs under a validated prefix; `checkoutFacts(revision)` returns the per-checkout HEAD reflog tip, tracked delta paths against the supplied commit, staged delta and porcelain bytes, excluding the platform-owned `.fgos` artifact; `remoteTracking(branch, remote)` returns upstream presence and the configured pinned remote URL. These are read-only driver operations, never an argv escape hatch. Node only translates these facts into existing consumer fields.

2026-10-20 (phase 07 unit 18): the remaining reader rows use typed Inspect facts. `diff` and `diffText` name their sides: two objects (`before` + `after`), a commit against the index (`before` + `index`, staged changes), a commit against the work tree (`before` + `worktree`), or the index against the work tree (`index` + `worktree`, unstaged changes); a checkout side requires a commit. `diffText` also takes bounded canonical literal paths (`--literal-pathspecs`) and an optional context-line count. `status` takes `ignoreSubmodules: 'dirty'`, which leaves a submodule's own dirt out and still reports a moved submodule commit. `commitTime(revision)` is a commit's committer time in seconds; `countCommits(include, exclude?)` counts the commits `include` reaches that `exclude` does not; `unmatchedCommitCount(upstream, head)` counts the non-merge commits of `head` with no patch-equivalent commit on `upstream` (`rev-list --cherry-pick --right-only --no-merges`); `trackedPaths` pages the index's repository-relative byte paths; `remoteUrl(remote)` is the existing redacted URL fact. Rust consumers open a repository with `GitDriver::open_at(path)`: a driver on this process's state root, and trust discovered on disk from `path` as for a cwd. `fgos version` and the Rust host show the first 12 hex digits of the full HEAD commit id; there is no abbreviation operation.

2026-10-20 (phase 07 unit 19): `status` takes `ignored: true`, which also lists ignored paths the way Git's `--ignored=traditional` names them (entry flag `ignored`): a wholly ignored directory is collapsed to `dir/`, and an ignored path inside a collapsed untracked directory is still listed on its own. The provisioning record of `fgos worktree create` lists untracked and ignored paths through it (`untracked: 'normal'`); `src/runner/worktree.mjs` runs no raw `ls-files`. Repository facts of a long-lived handle (effective config records, object format, ref storage) are cached per process, keyed by the git dir and revalidated by the existing ConfigStamp on every resolution, so each new port reuses them and formats are probed again exactly when the stamp changes; the config source list is keyed by the selected Git binary, `HOME` and `XDG_CONFIG_HOME`. Disk topology is still resolved on every call, and a checkout handle (ScopedCommit, Sync, Land checkout) starts from the same per-process entry for its git dir: a matching stamp answers config and formats with no Git child, a moved stamp reads them afresh, and what the operation proved is published back for the next handle. Measured in the unit-19 report: `approve --no-wait` against a held merge slot went from 79 to 21 Git spawns, `doctor --json` from 332 to 62.

2026-10-20 (phase 07 unit 14): the Land consumer's merge reads (`src/runner/merge.mjs`) and the merge verbs' ancestry checks use typed Inspect facts; no raw Git read remains in those files. `mergeTree(target, source)` (two commits) returns the merged tree OID and the deduplicated conflicted paths of `git merge-tree --write-tree`; Git exit 1 is the conflicted answer, anything else besides 0 is an error. Like that command it may add unreferenced objects to the object store and changes no ref, index or checkout. `listTree(revision, prefix)` exposes the existing driver tree listing: it pages, from the repository root, the entries of a commit or tree whose path starts with a literal byte prefix, with mode, type and object id. `attributes(revision, attribute, paths)` returns each literal path's value of one named attribute, read from the `.gitattributes` of that commit or tree (`check-attr --source`), as Git reports it (`unspecified`, `set`, `unset` or the value). `diff` accepts a commit or tree on either side, an optional literal path prefix, and `renames`: off (the default) both ends of a move are reported, on a rename is reported once under its new path with `from` naming the old one. `firstAncestryMerge(ancestor, descendant)` returns the earliest merge commit on the ancestry path between two commits, or none. All are bounded like the other Inspect reads and run with optional locks off.

Implementation may collapse ports internally; the appendix maps lessons to this surface. Idempotency is required wherever an effect can repeat.

**Current surface (2026-10-23).** The ports above are the surface; `packages/git/contracts/ports.v1.json` lists their operations and `test/git/native-load.test.mjs` pins that list against the addon, as `result-codes.v1.json` is pinned against `ResultCode` by `packages/git/rust/tests/contracts.rs`. The Node-reachable arms that nothing called are gone: Remote has `pushRefs` only (the Rust driver keeps `fetch_refs` and `inspect_remote`, which Event Store's Publication and the Rust tests call), the Publication facade has no `fetchRefs`, the RepositoryConfig facade has no `recover`, and there is no native-lock door (§11). The Checkpoint adapter stays until the Work lane removes its caller (`src/state/legacy-event-git-adapter.mjs`), and goes with it.

**One remote door.** `GitDriver::remote_target` (`packages/git/rust/src/remote.rs`) serves Remote, Publication and the sequence counters. It reads the configured URL raw, refuses control bytes, whitespace, `?`, `#`, `::`, a leading `-` in a user or host, and credentials, all on that raw string; compares it with the grant's pin; and names the transport. The pin is a display string and, for every URL the door accepts, equals the raw form. Git's scp form `[user@]host:path` is accepted as `ssh` through the same account rule, an `@` in its path's first segment is refused, and an `ssh://user@host/…` account name is kept in the pin because an account is not a secret (`https` userinfo and passwords stay refused and masked). The door reads and classifies only; Remote and Publication keep their own call halves because they run under different authorities. The sequence counter's home (`sequence_home` in `sequence.rs`) is decided from the config listing: no `origin` means the local counter; an `origin` that cannot be read or whose transport is unsupported is a named refusal before any claim, never a silent local counter.

Implementation checkpoint (2026-10-19): `src/git/refs.mjs` and `remote.mjs` marshal typed requests and explicit held handles, with no subprocess policy in Node. Refs uses worktree-admin 40 through membership revalidation, exact-old no-dereference CAS and read-back, and journals `RefUpdate`/`RefDelete` with a reachability pin and one TargetAttempt. `fastForwardCheckedOut` requires the exact registered worktree's rank-30 lease and a clean fast-forward. Non-event Remote takes literal source/destination pairs (fetch destinations under `refs/remotes/*`, push destinations under `refs/heads/*`), pins the granted remote URL, downloads objects before exact-old local CAS, and records `RemoteFetch`/`RemotePush` with per-target old/new values before TargetAttempt. Lost acknowledgements preserve the attempt and never resend that id. Unresolved operations preserve evidence and refuse replay. Linux files/reftable and local bare-remote proofs live in `packages/git/rust/tests/refs_remote.rs` and `test/git/{refs,remote,lock-handover}.test.mjs`. Separate-git-dir remains unsupported: inferred linked-cwd discovery requires a main `.git` directory and reciprocal registered admin/backlink, rather than trusting a worker gitfile alone.

2026-10-19 (phase 05 worktree, **not release-ready**): ScopedCommit, non-event main Checkpoint and Sync ports now have Rust implementations and mechanical addon/Node bindings. ScopedCommit records baseline/ref/dirty-content fingerprints, stages literal NUL pathspecs, records a pre-OID TargetAttempt, and checks the actual committed tree and path set after one hook invocation. A contract violation retains the actual OID. Sync journals its pinned target and operation-owned conflict/staged sets, returns a temporary resolver grant, checks conflict markers before staging, and checks both merge parents. The cutover hook delegates to the activated host's protocol-1 `git-grant-check`; that read-only route verifies the inherited main-50 borrower, WAL description binding, unspent TargetAttempt, exact deletion paths, current legacy ref and seal digest. Every cutover also stages the `.fgos/events` fence when it is present as a regular file, replacing the deleted event directory or, in a baseline-only repository, marking the cutover. Linux-x64 activation now requires the native addon to declare `git-wal-v1`; old manifests still deserialize for a named refusal. Phase-05 unit 2 carries a real pre-open acquisition reach floor through the private Node handle and opaque loan, and the release/dev-host/test addon producer emits its platform capabilities. Sync records a durable `sync-commit-attempt` (prepared tree) immediately before invoking commit and refuses an uncertain attempt on resume, even when HEAD matches; its resulting ref is synced before the outcome. ScopedCommit and Sync receipt replay derive the actual committed path set from the immutable commit, preserving hook contract violations. Parent-death barriers and genuine temporary hook indexes have Linux fixture evidence. Unit 3 restores declared producer bytes from a transaction-local pre-staging snapshot after an observed rejection with unchanged HEAD, including both move paths, clean declared files, symlinks and modes; the initially empty index is restored even when the hook staged extra paths. Uncertain/crashed attempts retain the WAL and refuse replay rather than guessing rollback. Adopted setsid holders use the holder-lock descriptor rule below. Installed-release main cutover and unrestricted host/cross-platform acceptance remain outstanding. See [unit-2 report](../../../plans/reports/phase-lead-261019-1654-git-p05-u2-handover-reach-and-capability.md).

2026-10-19 (landing repair): RepositoryConfig is the single source for publication fetch mappings. Event Store and Node setup/doctor read the Rust policy through the existing crate/addon boundary. The legacy mapping is `+refs/fgos/legacy*:refs/fgos/remotes/<remote>/legacy*`: Git permits an absent wildcard source, including before the cutover, and the empty suffix maps `refs/fgos/legacy` to exactly `refs/fgos/remotes/<remote>/legacy`. ensureFetchMapping replaces an installed old exact legacy mapping in the same journaled, native-config-lock-protected update, including when both forms exist; unrelated config and push settings remain untouched. Setup/doctor use `eventStore.remote` (project over global, registered setup default `origin`), and also detect and migrate obsolete exact mappings on `origin` when the configured remote differs. `obsolete_legacy_fetch_mapping` owns the obsolete form for every consumer; callers do not infer it by policy-list index. The wildcard reserves all `refs/fgos/legacy?*` names (any nonempty suffix): writers must never create them. Doctor reports any corresponding `refs/fgos/remotes/<remote>/legacy?*` mirrors, with source/mirror cleanup guidance; it never deletes these refs automatically.

2026-10-19 (review fixes): Untrusted repository URL rewrites (`url.*`, including `insteadOf` and `pushInsteadOf`) refuse before invocation. Remote overrides generic HTTP proxy, TLS verification, extra headers and redirect policy with safe values, and refuses other repository-local HTTP/HTTPS, SSH, credential, protocol and proxy overrides, including URL-scoped HTTP settings; trusted global transport configuration remains outside this repository boundary. Trust is determined by `git config --show-scope`: only system/global/command scopes are trusted; local/worktree and any unknown scope are untrusted, regardless of origin path spelling. Both the trusted main checkout and a linked handle’s worktree configuration are checked. Doctor’s `git-repository-config` reports refusing settings, including actions/checkout’s local `http.<url>.extraheader`; CI uses `persist-credentials: false` plus a trusted global credential helper or `GIT_CONFIG_GLOBAL`-level setting. Doctor never migrates secrets. Trusted global credential helpers remain available to Remote/Publication; preflight still refuses repository-local helpers, and the driver still scrubs inherited `GIT_*` overrides. SSH URLs may select a username (`ssh://git@host/path`); password/userinfo credentials are refused. Git's scp form `[user@]host:path` is accepted as ssh, but the bracketed IPv6 scp form is refused, although Git accepts it. Push uses `--atomic`, never forces, and classifies every destination from post-transfer observations; all unchanged destinations establish rejection, mixed observations remain unknown. Fetch refreshes remote-tracking refs with Git's default forced-update semantics, protected by exact-old no-dereference CAS; ancestry is not required. All destinations are checked before the first install. A stale destination or failed install with no local effect closes with a terminal not-acknowledged Transfer and per-target observations, without a pending operation id; readable partial installs close as unknown with observations. Unreadable outcomes retain recovery evidence and never replay. A failed fetch CAS does not stop the remaining destination installs; the final observations cover all destinations. A server without atomic push support produces a terminal Rejected outcome; there is no non-atomic fallback.

`fastForwardCheckedOut` refuses the trusted main checkout. Membership includes rebase-merge/rebase-apply `head-name` and `BISECT_START`; an ordinary user worktree lock does not make registration uncertain, while `locked initializing` does. Doctor `git-worktree-recovery` classifies a lock for inspection only (2026-10-19): a lock whose reason names `pid <N>` where process N is live and started no later than the lock file (pid plus start time, so a reused pid does not count) is `locked-by-live-process`, advisory like `dirty-or-stale-checkout-preserved`; a lock naming a dead, reused or unparseable owner stays `locked-registration` and fails the row. The reason text is read only for that pid token, never as authority for anything else, and nothing is unlocked or repaired. An explicit ignored-path/incoming-tree collision check preserves ignored content (Git 2.53 dry-run alone did not refuse the measured replacement). After index refresh, a dry-run two-tree `read-tree -m -n -u` must pass before TargetAttempt. A failed ref write which still observes the expected old tip is a write failure with recovery evidence, never a claimed CAS loss. RemoteFetch/RemotePush are the sole transfer WAL kinds, with explicit target old/new values for both Publication and non-event Remote; Publication retains its authority, expected/next and no-resend recovery rules. Historical Publication records without target detail deserialize with an empty target list for conservative recovery; all new writers emit the targets field and non-event transfers require a complete target set.

2026-10-19 (phase 03): Worktree uses the existing Rust LockScope, worktree-admin 40, Inspect topology, hardened driver, WAL admission/TargetAttempt and reachability pins. One hold spans identity/custody/status/proof checks and remove/move. Git children borrow admin authority; the caller's verified custody description stays pinned in the seeded scope until return. Remove preserves staged, unstaged, untracked, ignored and reappearing worker-state bytes, native locks, live cwd and unknown process facts; a process that exits during the cwd scan is re-observed through Process before treating its missing cwd as clear. A successful create strips worker `.fgos`; intentionally absent tracked state paths are marked skip-worktree in the new checkout's index, so later cleanup does not need an exemption for dirty paths. No provisioning callback runs in Git. Detached session allocation owns an `mkdtemp` directory and only attempts an empty-directory rollback before registration; registered cleanup always goes through non-force Git removal.

2026-10-19 (landing repair): A tracked, clean `.fgos` with no skip-worktree entries is not reappearing worker state and does not prevent Worktree removal or relocation.

2026-10-19 (phase 03 host gate): An unreadable cwd does not refuse remove or relocate; live same-user or unknown-owner processes with unreadable cwds produce a stderr warning naming pid and comm. A readable cwd inside or below the checkout still refuses. The caller's verified dispatch-cwd lease and refusal of another holder's lease cover fgOS producers; status checks preserve dirty or uncertain trees. Unreadability alone is not evidence that a process occupies this checkout.

2026-10-19 (phase 03 review): Allocation inside the trusted main checkout (including ignored `.claude/worktrees`) is supported; equal/ancestor paths, the common Git directory and nesting in linked checkouts remain refused. Readable cwd facts from any user protect a checkout; unreadable cwd facts are judged only for the effective user or unknown ownership. Privileged actors and unreadable foreign-user processes are outside this guarantee, so shared cross-user checkout custody is not certified. Same-user unreadability is advisory (see the host-gate policy below). Remove/move preflight refuses user locks and submodules and repeats custody/cwd checks immediately before TargetAttempt and the Git call. Removal additionally requires `deliveredTo` reachable from a surviving named non-`refs/fgos` ref; a detached tip alone is not delivery. Ignored bytes remain protected; ignored-directory inspection is collapsed with `--directory` and bounded by the Inspect output ceiling. Doctor reports preserved dirty/stale checkouts as advisory; pending operations, missing/locked registrations and partial/untrusted admin entries fail the row.

Under worktree-admin, after an attempted Git refusal or during repair, observable non-effects settle as terminal `Worktree { completed: false, details }` with the checked paths/registration observations, then release the candidate pin. Create requires absent registration and absent or empty owned destination; move requires absent destination plus unchanged admin identity, reciprocal source backlink and expected tip; remove requires that unchanged source state. This is record-only reconciliation under §10 rule 4, never a repeated attempt or a claim that an attempted operation was abandoned before attempt (§10 rule 4b). A replay of a false outcome returns a terminal refusal. Ambiguous residue remains pending and preserved.


Worktree WAL kinds bind canonical paths and registration names; move/remove additionally bind the admin directory's `dev:ino`, and remove binds the consumer's expected tip, delivery target and evidence digest. An established id is replayed without repeating its effect; pending attempts retain evidence. Recovery repairs a move only at its recorded destination and checks that destination's gitfile. An initializing create lock requires its own attempted WAL record, matching registration, branch and tip; a partial or dirty checkout stays preserved. Prune accepts only attempted WorktreeRemove evidence, a missing recorded checkout, the same admin inode and a matching backlink (or a partially removed backlink); it removes only the validated Git admin remainder, preserving unknown files, symlinks and native locks. It never invokes global prune or acquires custody. Four Linux x86_64 crash fixtures kill actual Git at ptrace syscall barriers; power-loss durability is not claimed.

`fgos worktree create <path> [--branch <name> | --detach] [--base <ref>] [--json]` is available now under the owner's plan Q2 answer of 2026-10-18. Without a branch it creates a detached checkout. After the port returns, the verb provisions the new checkout through the runner's own helpers (`provisionDependencies`, then the project's `worktreeSetup.commands` with `FGOS_REPO_ROOT` set to the main checkout), outside every Git lock; a failing setup command fails the verb and leaves the created checkout in place. This is the one door: workflow and `fgos run --worktree` worktrees are made by this verb or by the runner's own creators, which already provision. Base accepts a full OID, HEAD or a literal branch/tag/remote ref. `fgos worktree remove <path> --delivered-to <ref> [--json]` (owner-approved 2026-10-20) wires the port's guarded remove: it resolves the worktree's current tip as `expectedTip`, resolves the delivery target (an object id, or a name through the same grammar as `--base`: full ref, branch, tag or remote-tracking name, resolved by the Inspect door's one literal-ref resolver) to `deliveredTo`, and passes the sha256 of the delivery statement `{path, expectedTip, deliveredTo: {ref, oid}}` as `evidenceDigest`. The port refuses a dirty checkout, a live process, preserved worker state, a tip that is not an ancestor of (or equal to) `deliveredTo`, or a `deliveredTo` no non-internal ref contains; its error code and message surface unchanged and there is no force flag. Before the port runs, the verb sets aside exactly what create's provisioning placed (a record under the machine state root: each copied file with its digest, the installed `node_modules` declared regenerable), puts it back if the port refuses and deletes it once the checkout is gone; a worker-edited copy or any other ignored byte stays and the port's ignored-bytes refusal is unchanged. Other production Worktree callers migrate together in phase 07. `git-worktree-recovery` reads raw admin entries (including partial entries omitted by Git listing), dirty/stale checkouts and pending Worktree WAL records without repairing; phase 04 adds its producing-custody row to this same diagnostic.

### Recovery and GitHub adapter contract (phase 06 units 9–13)

`createGitRecovery(options)` exposes `inspectOperation({operationId})`,
`listPending({bound?})`, `listRetainedLand({bound?})` (finished Land chains whose candidate is still retained) and `reconcile({operationId}, held?)`. Inspection
returns the request digest, payload kind, terminal state and retained intent;
compacted operations return their retained request digest and terminal outcome.
Pending scans return `{operations, complete}`; corruption refuses by operation
id rather than silently omitting evidence. Unsupported payload variants refuse
with their variant name through WAL decoding. Reconciliation delegates to the
owning port and returns its Rust result (a receipt for Land/Publication, an
outcome for ref/transfer/ScopedCommit, the existing result for Restore/Sync,
and inspection after Worktree repair). A compacted operation returns its
terminal WAL outcome without introducing any new effect. Recovery holds are
released before returning to consumer settlement; no consumer state is written.

ConfigChange reconciliation delegates to the existing config recovery under
repo-config 80, without acquiring the peer-rank recovery leaf. This resource
authority excludes fgOS config writers and recoverers; native config.lock
excludes ordinary Git writers. Caller loans are refused on ConfigChange rather
than silently dropped. The interrupted-operation matrix covers all twelve
payload kinds at durable Intent in both recovery orders; additional ref/transfer
rows cover pin installation, TargetAttempt and observed CAS.

Publication recovery retains its existing event-seal resource authority without
taking the recovery leaf, including live Event Store publish/recover paths.
Other recovery paths take recovery 80 last; it never substitutes for resource
authority. Git reuses the registered recovery lock with
`runDir=<common>/fgos/git`, hence `<common>/fgos/git/locks/recovery.lock`.
Non-event transfers retain their lock-free scope (caller ranks below 60);
worktree-admin belongs only to their observe-only recovery, not transfer admission.

GitHub API merge stays in the GitHub adapter under **consumer authorization**;
Git ports do not grant approval or perform API merges. A merge timeout is
**outcome-unknown until queried**, including the adapter's existing
`blocked` transport result: it does not prove the server declined the merge.
The production adapter queries PR state/merge commit after an unreachable merge
result, returns the observed merged receipt when available, otherwise retains
`blocked` with `reason: outcome-unknown`. A repeated merge call first queries
state and returns an existing MERGED result without sending another request.
The contract test invokes the production merge adapter twice.
Any local mutation the adapter needs must go through the typed Git ports.
Phase 06 added the timeout-query and MERGED guard to the existing adapter.

## 4. Publication port: refs/fgos/*

Event Store's crate-internal driver (event-store spec §3/§20) moves behind this port **without a protocol change**. Node reaches publication through the **combined N-API addon** (below) with **no exception** to the exec guard under the `state` lock. Git is forbidden under state 60. Event Store bounded append/read inside state remains its own in-process/open-log path; no DAG walk or Git call there.

**One Node load: one combined addon (owner decision Q1, topology (a)).** A Node process reaches exactly one in-process instance of `fgos-git`. Two independent Rust cdylib addons cannot share one in-process crate instance, so the Node payload ships **one combined N-API addon** that links both `fgos-event-store` and `fgos-git` and exports both facades. Event Store's seal/sync publication and every Node-reachable Git operation run through that one load. There is no per-operation host-process route (`fgos internal git <op>`) for Git and no JS-bridged second addon; topologies (b) and (c) are not adopted. A second, separate addon load of either crate in the same Node process fails closed, and doctor reports one ABI row for the combined addon. This changes Event Store's packaging (event-store spec §17, §21: its own addon of `fgos-event-store` becomes the combined addon); the signing bundle (2026-10-18) made that amendment, with the Publication port placement in event-store §3, §4 and §20. Rust consumers (observe, `fgos-work-state`) link `fgos-git` in-process. Reversal trigger: if experiment E3 shows the combined addon fails a budget stated before the run, or fails rollback with live processes (P-RELEASE), Q1 reopens.

Operations: writeBlob(bytes), writeTree(literal entries/modes/OIDs), writeCommit(tree, ordered parents, identity/message), readRef/Object, walkCommits(tips,cursor,bounds), compareAndSwap(ref,expected,next,operationId), fetchRefs and pushRefs. No index, worktree, HEAD, main-checkout lock, or consumer semantics.

**C1 — distinct execution policy.** Publication operates on direct namespace-qualified refs. It refuses symbolic refs in owned and mirror namespaces, validates the destination, and uses a backend mode that cannot follow a symbolic ref outside the grant even if the ref changes after preflight. A plain textual prefix check is insufficient. On the Git floor measured (2.53.0) a plain `update-ref` on a symbolic `refs/fgos/*` ref wrote through to `refs/heads/victim`, and `--no-deref` kept the victim unchanged but silently replaced the symbolic ref with a direct one while the expected-old check passed against the dereferenced value. Two separate checks therefore apply: (1) containment, the guarantee: every ref mutation runs in no-dereference mode with the expected OID on the actual update, so it never changes a ref outside `refs/fgos/*` even when the ref turns symbolic after preflight (the P-SYM-HOOK re-check confirmed this on 2.53.0: every `refs/heads/*` ref unchanged across the attack window, while the control without `--no-deref` moved the victim); (2) refusal, best effort: the ref is read as a symbolic-or-direct fact at preflight and again at read-back, and a symbolic ref found at either point is refused or reported as `recovery-required` tamper evidence. Detection of a **transient** substitution is not guaranteed: the same re-check turned a direct ref symbolic between preflight and the CAS, the `--no-deref` CAS overwrote it with a direct ref at the candidate, and both reads saw a direct ref, so read-back cannot recover the overwritten symbolic state. A substitution that is still present at read-back is detected. No detection mechanism for the transient case is claimed until a proof names one. An expected-OID match is never evidence that the ref was direct. Object writes and reads do not enable replace-object or alternate-object authority from worker input, and every identity/type/tree/ancestry/publication read runs with replacement objects disabled (`--no-replace-objects` or its environment equivalent): scrubbing `GIT_REPLACE_REF_BASE` does not disable repository-owned `refs/replace/*`, which made `cat-file` return a different commit for the same OID in the floor proof. Worker-supplied alternate-object, replace-ref and shallow authority (`GIT_ALTERNATE_OBJECT_DIRECTORIES`, `GIT_REPLACE_REF_BASE`, `--reference`-style overrides, config injected through the scrubbed env context of G-001/G-183) is always refused. Repository-owned layout in the trusted common dir (`.git/objects/info/alternates`, shallow, promisor remotes) is a compatibility profile: until E5 establishes which of these configurations `fgos-git` supports, an operation that needs an object unavailable under that layout refuses by name rather than fetching implicitly, and the refusal names the layout feature. Raw Git does not do this by itself: a read of a missing blob in a promisor partial clone spawned a lazy `fetch`, and a missing alternate gave a generic exit 128. So the hardened context sets no-lazy-fetch (`GIT_NO_LAZY_FETCH=1` on the measured floor) for every non-Remote operation (status, diff, read, checkout, merge, not only readObject); shallow, alternates and promisor layout are detected before invocation; a missing object or incomplete traversal becomes a named layout refusal that keeps the actual Git error. Any fetch or deepen is a Remote operation with its own authorization, never an Inspect repair.

Publication's private Git calls **cannot execute repository hooks**, including the **reference-transaction** hook, nor inherit worker-provided external commands. “No hook-running commit porcelain” is not enough: plain `update-ref` ran `reference-transaction` (`prepared`, `committed`) on the measured floor. Every Publication call carries a trusted hook-disabled configuration (`core.hooksPath` set by the backend to an fgOS-owned empty location; `/dev/null` suppressed the hook on 2.53.0) that no repository or worker config can override. This does not disable guards on ScopedCommit or checkout mutations. Transport helpers and credential providers are Remote capabilities, not implicit privileges of writeBlob/readObject/CAS.

Use explicit trusted common-dir plumbing. Validate object types and refs. Event Store provides the seal/merge manifests and parent order; Git never computes event union or business conflict outcomes.

Namespace grants are explicit: own events/local-events/branch-events and authorized one-time legacy import; mirrors only fetch-written; Git operation pins reserved to Git. CAS distinguishes absent from exact OID and returns actual tip on loss. No event segment deletion follows a Git receipt alone: Event Store still verifies coverage and durably records its own sealed receipt.

Required unchanged Event Store protocol:

1. Seal/sync owns event-seal 58; event-append 70 protects rotation only and is released before Git.
2. Write trees/commits, journal intent, CAS `refs/fgos/events/<machine-id>`; retain segments on CAS loss. Event Store retries by its section 10 rules.
3. Walk both parents of a DAG with visited set and bounded continuation. Only Event Store decides certified coverage pruning. Incomplete traversal never claims complete coverage.
4. Fetch `+refs/fgos/events/*:refs/fgos/remotes/<remote>/events/*` and the existing legacy mirror mapping. `+` is allowed only for mirror refresh as Event Store §14 requires. Never overwrite own ref by fetch.
5. Push only own events and authorized legacy ref; never force-push, mirrors, local/branch streams or Git pins. Non-FF triggers Event Store fetch/reconcile using a two-parent commit then ordinary push. No Git-side event reconciliation.

**CAS and pin ordering (V-CAS fold).** A multi-ref `update-ref --stdin` transaction is **not** crash-atomic on the `files` backend: the Git floor proof killed one after the target rename and left target = next, pin = expected and `pin.lock` on disk. Reftable was exercised only for completed transactions. So Git never relies on multi-ref atomicity: the operation pin is written by exact-old CAS and read back as a prepare substep, the prepared WAL record is made durable, the TargetAttempt record of §10 is made durable, and only then is the target moved by a single-ref exact-old CAS. This order is mandatory and has no optimization: Git never puts a target publication in a multi-ref transaction, with the pin or with anything else (the M1 and V-CAS re-checks showed that combining the first pin install with the target publishes before a read-back pin and a durable prepared record). Recovery still admits every observed subset of a multi-ref transaction's effects and any retained native ref lock, because legacy writers and raw Git leave such residue; that admission never authorizes Git to write one. The pin is reachability against GC, not the receipt. The file WAL record remains the authority for intent, phase and attribution, and recovery reconciles target and pin from it (§10).

**Non-publication Node→Rust route** (commit-scoped, land, worktree): the same combined addon, used **outside** state 60, never an exec-guard exception (owner decision Q1). Lock-taking operations through it use LockScope option (a) of §5. Panelist 2's host route is not shipped; E3 measures the chosen route against the current Node writers and is the reversal trigger named above.

The Node `createGitPublication` constructor has no production caller (Event Store publishes in process through the crate); only the proof scripts under `scripts/git/` use it.

## 5. Layering and unchanged 4.3-R locks

Reuse Holder-lock's registry, existing paths/ranks, kernel drivers, description borrowing, predecessor domain and fences **unchanged**. No TTL/heartbeat/PID-based authority, inode deletion/recreation or new lock map.

Every mutating request declares a canonical resource set and the **existing** authority required for that set. Normal execution and recovery acquire the same resource authority. Publication of an Event Store stream uses event-seal; branch land uses merge-slot and, where needed, worktree-admin/main-checkout; worktree effects require the applicable admin/main authority and continuous Execution custody. A supervisor recovery lock may serialize the supervisor; it **never** substitutes for these authorities and is not a repository-wide journal mutex. Recovery determines needed resources before acquiring locks. Unsupported or missing authority refuses mutation.

A fresh Rust scope must not hide the Node caller's held locks. Unknown mutation posture fails closed. Holder-lock §9 defines LockScope as a Node object that acquires kernel locks by spawning `fgos internal lock-fd` with the fd in stdio slot 3; it defines no cross-language handle, and none is added.

**LockScope option (a) (owner decision Q1).** For lock-taking operations (commit-scoped, sync, land, worktree, restore), Rust `fgos-git` acquires the ranks it needs (20/40/50) in its **own Rust LockScope**. The Node caller passes, as explicit parameters of the operation, the ranks its scope holds and a descriptor for each held lock the operation relies on (for example the `dispatch-cwd` lease that is the custody token). The Rust scope seeds its rank floor from those held ranks, so the Holder-lock rule "acquire only above every held rank" applies across the N-API boundary exactly as inside one Node scope. A passed descriptor is never trusted as declared: Git verifies it with the three-condition borrower test of holder-lock §5 (named lock, `fstat` `dev:ino` equal to the lock file, `LOCK_EX | LOCK_NB` on that description succeeds) before any effect and again before the irreversible boundary. A held-rank list without a verified descriptor confers no authority, and a set of rank numbers alone is never a lock (no bitmap). The borrower test proves only that the description is usable and exclusive **at the moment of the test**: the model-check probe opened a free lock file, matched name and inode, and `LOCK_EX | LOCK_NB` succeeded because it had just acquired the lock (holder-lock §19 names this case). So a descriptor is accepted only as an acquired handle of the caller's Holder-lock scope in the same process, passed through the combined addon as an opaque handle, never as an fd number or path taken from request input; handles are close-only, a revoked or closed handle is refused, and a successful try-lock never proves continuous custody or a generation handoff (§6). Git children receive a verified lease only through `run_borrowed`. Rust-acquired locks are released by closing their descriptions before the operation returns, so the Node scope never sees a lock it did not take. Publication is unaffected: under seal 58 it never acquires 20/40/50.

| Rank | Resource / rule |
|---|---|
| 20 | Existing per-target merge-slot; reserve before expensive land work; no-wait handled here |
| 30 | Existing `dispatch-cwd` lease, held by Execution and extended across the runner commit; the custody token Git validates. **Not** a Git lock and never acquired by Git. See N7. |
| 40 | Existing worktree-admin. Holder-lock §16 (amended 2026-10-17, owner decision Q6) wraps add, remove, relocate (move) and prune. Because it serializes worktree registration, Git also holds it across the checkout revalidation, CAS and read-back of every Refs mutation of `refs/heads/*` (§3) and of Land publication (§9 step 4); holder-lock §16 leaves to this section which Git operations take it. |
| 50 | Existing main-checkout, only short actual main index/tree/HEAD mutation/recovery |
| 55 | Git-native index.lock, never an fgOS implementation |
| 58 | Event Store event-seal; publication cannot acquire 20/40/50 beneath it |
| 60 | Consumer state; no Git, child, verify/build or lower-rank acquisition |
| 70 | Event Store event-append; no Git while held |
| 80 | Existing leaves such as recovery, only after all needed lower ranks; release before later state 60 settlement |

The common order remains 20→40→50→60. Operations take only needed locks. Verify/build never runs under 50/60. Publish revalidates under appropriate locks and performs no arbitrary Node callback. Consumer settlement follows release. Recovery requiring main takes 20/40/50 before recovery 80; publication recovery without checkout takes no main/admin and remains compatible with caller seal 58. No invented publication lock rank.

**worktree-admin wrap set (owner decision Q6).** G-042 and G-155 require dirty-check through destroy under one ownership. Holder-lock §16 is amended (2026-10-17) so that `worktree-admin` (rank 40) wraps add, remove, relocate (move) and prune: the dirty-check, the destroy or move, and the registration change run under one hold of 40. It is still one lock; Git callers **must not invent a second admin lock**. The amendment's implementation lands with the Git component. Read-only listing is not a globally serialized exclusive operation and takes no lock.

**Rank-30 versus rank-20 legality (R30; owner-decided structure; M2 held in the model abstraction, §19).** Rank 30 is the `dispatch-cwd` lease (holder-lock §7), taken by a dispatch on its worktree in the runner's per-dispatch LockScope above `runner` 10 and, once it exists, `attempt` 15. Under Q2 it is held from producer start through the runner commit and handoff; under Q1 the Rust scope inherits the caller's held ranks, so the Holder-lock rule "acquire only above every held rank" binds Git exactly as it binds the Node scope. Every rank below is an existing registry row; the table invents none.

Execution rejects a mutating Unit that resolves to the main checkout before opening a native Git port or acquiring/borrowing custody; this policy refusal takes precedence over Git lock-admission errors. Resume reports a live cwd holder through the same coded `dispatch-in-flight` refusal as dispatch. A controller crash after native commit return leaves the real persisted ScopedCommit intent pending; resume recovers that intent and settles the original receipt without launching the producer or committing again. Runner regressions: `test/runner/execution/run.test.mjs` and `run-resume-order.test.mjs`.

| Operation | Locks Git acquires (in order) | May already be held by the caller | Illegal combination and how the protocol avoids it |
|---|---|---|---|
| ScopedCommit in a linked worktree (runner commit) | none; Git's own `index.lock` 55 inside the git child | 10, 15, 30 (required: verified `dispatch-cwd` lease on that worktree, borrowed into the git children) | Missing or stale 30 fails closed. Git never acquires 20 or 40 here, so holding 30 is never an inversion. |
| ScopedCommit or checkpoint in the main checkout | 50 | 10, 15, 20, 30, 40 | Holding 30 then taking 50 is legal. Holding 60 or 70 is illegal (rank rule; no Git under state). Holder-lock `run_borrowed` maps one description per child: a git child that must carry both a 30 lease and 50 needs a second mapped slot, which Holder-lock does not define; until Holder-lock is amended for that, the combination is refused and the runner commit runs in a linked worktree only. |
| Sync start/continue/abort (producer worktree, pinned target OID) | none (target is pinned by OID, so no 20) | 10, 15, 30 (required) | Sync never takes 20: catchup reads and pins the target tip and does not publish it. Missing or stale 30 fails closed. |
| Land prepare/publish (merge or squash into a target ref) | 20, then 40 for candidate create, release 40, then 40 and 50 for publication | 10, 15 only | **Land needing 20 while holding 30 is an inversion.** The protocol avoids it: land never runs inside a dispatch scope. The runner commit and handoff end, the `dispatch-cwd` lease is released, and land is entered from a scope that holds no rank above 15 (the runner's merge path or a merge verb). A land request whose held ranks include 30 or anything above 20 is refused with `lock-order-violation` before any effect. Land does not need the producer's 30: it works in its own detached candidate worktree. |
| Worktree create / createDetached | 40, unless the caller lends its own verified worktree-admin of this repository (2026-10-20, phase 07: the waiting `addWorktree` door holds it; nothing above 40 may be held with it) | 10, 15, 20, 30 (when the caller holds another worktree's lease), 40 (lent, as stated) | Taken below 50, never while 50 or 60 is held. The new worktree's own 30 does not exist yet (its key is `dev:ino` of the new directory); Execution takes it after create returns, outside every Git lock. |
| Worktree remove / relocate (move) | 40, unless lent as for create | 10, 15, 20, 30 (required when the worktree has a live custody holder: the caller's verified lease on that worktree), 40 (lent) | 30 then 40 is legal. Removing or moving a worktree whose 30 is held by another holder is refused, never forced. |
| Worktree prune / repairRegistration | 40, unless lent as for create | 10, 15, 20, 40 (lent) | Prune never acquires 30. Prune acts only on registrations whose directory is missing; a directory that exists is handled by remove under the rule above. |
| SafeRestore (producer worktree) | none | 10, 15, 30 (required) | Missing or stale 30 fails closed. |
| SafeRestore (main checkout) | 50 | 10, 15, 20, 40 | Holding 60 or 70 is illegal. |
| Refs ensureBranch/advanceRef/deleteRef/fastForwardCheckedOut on `refs/heads/*` (changed by the 2026-10-18 re-check fold; was "none") | 40, held from the checkout revalidation through the exact-old CAS and read-back | 10, 15, 20, 30 (fastForwardCheckedOut requires the verified 30 on the one worktree that holds the branch) | Holding 40, 50, 60 or 70 is refused with `lock-order-violation` before any effect, so 40 is never taken under main 50 (order 20→40→50). Only Worktree borrows a lent 40; Refs, Land and Remote always take their own. Exact-old CAS serializes the ref value; 40 serializes checkout membership, which the CAS does not see. A branch checked out in any worktree is refused (§3) except under fastForwardCheckedOut. Post-land branch cleanup runs after land, outside the dispatch scope. Refs on `refs/fgos/*` are Publication (row below), never checked out, and take no lock. |
| Publication (Event Store seal/sync, `refs/fgos/*`) | none (Event Store holds event-seal 58) | anything below 58 | Never 20/40/50 under 58; never under 60 or 70. |
| Remote fetch/push of non-event refs | none | anything below 60 | No Git under 60. |
| RepositoryConfig (fetch mapping and hooks) | repo-config 80 | anything below 60; recovery refuses caller loans | The resource authority serializes fgOS writers and ConfigChange recoverers; native config.lock excludes ordinary Git config writers. ConfigChange recovery takes no peer-rank recovery leaf. |
| Recovery of an interrupted operation | owning resource authority, then `recovery` 80 last; non-event transfer recovery uses 40 then 80; Publication uses only caller event-seal 58; ConfigChange uses only repo-config 80 | as for that operation; non-event transfer recovery below 40; ConfigChange no caller loans | Recovery 80 never replaces resource authority and is released before any later state 60 settlement. |

The table is the structure the owner chose (Q1 option (a), Q2 lease extension). M2 (unit-run-1792254306261-e7f3b8ba) enumerated every subset of the 11 ranks against 26 operation and recovery profiles and 5 descriptor states (266,240 cases) and found no two-scope wait cycle, including the refusal of a land request that carries 30. That is a check of the abstraction, not of fairness, bounded waiting, cancellation, alias identity or descendant drain, and the hook was encoded as a single probe; V-ORDER still has to exercise the real Node→addon→Rust→hook ladder. M2 ran with the Refs row taking no lock; its new rank-40 profile is legal by the rank rule (40 above every rank it may be held with) and is re-checked in §19.

Borrow via existing `run_borrowed` only: exact child fd mapping, CLOEXEC restored, inode/description try-lock borrower check, close-only release, gc/maintenance auto off, wait for child domain empty. Text env/PID grants do not confer authority. Hook strangers probe once and never wait while holding index 55. Activity beacon is advisory, never a lock.

Monotonic bounded wait budget covers retries. Retry only named contention, not lock-order/corruption/unknown protocol errors. Cancellation/timeout uses Process containment, not a process-group kill alone: in the Git floor proof a hook and a clean filter started a child that called `setsid()`, and that child survived `killpg` of the Git group both under a deadline and after Git exited 0. A group kill is a guarantee only for members that stay in the group. Descendant drain is claimed only where a Process containment domain whose driver answers `Empty` exists (process spec §4-5: `linux-gate`; Windows `job-object` per holder-lock §8.1); elsewhere, after the direct Git child exits or is killed, Git reports `containment-unproven` as `recovery-required` for that operation and its worktree, keeps the WAL evidence, and never infers that the domain is empty or that a successor may enter. Holder-lock's `run_borrowed` leftover kill (holder-lock §8: on Linux every process still holding the lock description, by identity) still runs; it covers escapees that kept the description, not those that closed it. Git installs no process-wide handler that kills Node. Post-effect cancellation records/reports pending recovery. Platform guarantee and macOS containment limits inherit Holder-lock; unsupported Windows/filesystem posture is a named failure until its driver is actually activated/proven.

## 6. N7 and RUL84

**N7 — ownership and mechanism decided (owner decision Q2).** Execution owns continuous custody from producer start through attempt exit, runner commit, receipt and handoff. Worker exit never releases custody. Git validates it and owns the mutation, not attempt scheduling. Successor producer/reset/sync/remove/commit is refused until current generation handoff is proven. Missing/stale custody fails closed. Read-only workflows require no writable custody.

**Mechanism: the existing `dispatch-cwd` lease, extended across the runner commit.** There is no new Custody entity (design principle 1). The lease Execution already takes on the worktree (rank 30, holder-lock §7, runner RUL101) is no longer released at the end of the assignment; it is held through the runner commit, its receipt and the handoff, and only then released. Contract:

- Execution passes the lease to Git as a held rank plus its descriptor (§5, LockScope option (a)). Git verifies the descriptor by the holder-lock §5 borrower test before any effect and again before the irreversible boundary; a missing, unverifiable or stale lease (wrong `dev:ino`, a description that no longer holds the lock, a worktree whose identity changed) fails closed with a named cause.
- Git children of the operation (commit, hooks) receive the lease through `run_borrowed`, like any other borrowed lock, so a dying Node caller does not free the worktree while git still writes in it.
- Git never acquires, extends or releases the lease; Execution does.
- The change to the run supervisor and to the lease lifetime (closing the gap named in holder-lock §19, "released before the runner commits the Unit's writes") is implementation work owned by the Git plan. This spec states the contract only and does not amend holder-lock §19.
- Predecessor-domain emptiness on Linux/macOS is **not** an existing guarantee (holder-lock §19); that assumption stays currently **false** until the attempt lock exists.

**Successor admission after controller death (M3 / P-CUSTODY fold).** The model check found the shortest violation in two steps, “controller dies → successor acquires free lease”, and reproduced it with a real kernel `flock`: a producer descendant that did not hold the lease description kept writing in the worktree while the successor held the lease. A git child that holds a borrowed description is excluded correctly; a producer process is not, because it holds no copy. So a free `dispatch-cwd` lease is **not** proof of generation handoff. Rule:

- Successor producer, reset, sync, remove or commit on a worktree may start only when the previous custody generation ended by a recorded handoff (lease closed by Execution after the receipt), **or** the previous holder's whole process domain is proven empty by the holder-lock §8 predecessor rule: the custody holder declares the producer's containment domain in the lease's predecessor record before the producer starts, and the successor, after it holds the lease, applies holder-lock §8 steps 2-3 unchanged (kill a non-empty domain at once, then proceed only when Process `domain_is_empty` answers `Empty`; otherwise `held (predecessor-domain-not-empty)`). Execution applies this rule when it acquires the lease for a successor, because it owns custody (Q2); Git still only validates the lease it is handed.
- Where no domain was declared, or the domain kind has no driver that answers `Empty` (today: Linux and macOS until the attempt lock and run-supervisor containment exist; `macos-tree` answers `Unknown`; Linux without a PID namespace is best-effort), admission **fails closed** with `custody-predecessor-unknown`. It never assumes the domain is empty. The worktree and its bytes are preserved and reported by `git-worktree-recovery`; Execution may dispatch the successor in a **new** worktree, so a refused worktree does not stop the item.
- The producer itself never receives the lease description: any copy may `LOCK_UN` (holder-lock §5 rule 6), and a descendant that closes its fds escapes anyway. Inheriting the fd is therefore not the safety mechanism; the domain proof is. Handing the lease to the attempt gate, which runs inside the domain, is the holder-lock §6 lease form and is how the domain proof and the lease coincide once the gate exists.

- **Recorded handoff over a best-effort domain (owner decision 2026-10-19).** After a successful run Execution clears its own predecessor record once the producer's domain is observed empty (holder-lock §8 step 0). For `linux-pgroup` and `macos-tree` that observation cannot see a `setsid` or double-fork escapee, so the handoff does not cover one; on supervised Linux the attempt gate (a subreaper) kills such escapees before the receipt. Refusing to clear best-effort records was rejected: it would strand the worktree after every unconfined Linux or macOS run.

No claim of concurrent commit safety until the lease extension and this admission rule are implemented and V-CUSTODY passes on the implementation. The M3/P-CUSTODY re-check (§19) held only in a finite model and a fixture probe: the old witness is now refused with `custody-predecessor-unknown`.

**RUL84.** The lowest shared **write-scope foundation leaf** owns the grammar/matcher/expansion; Execution owns granted paths; Git enforces them. One Rust implementation and one port; no Node matcher plus a Rust copy. It may initially be a leaf module of the Git package but imports no operations or consumer. Replace `src/runner/dispatch/write-scope.mjs` with a thin facade in the same switch, preserving exports/golden semantics. Packaging (leaf vs independent crate) follows the route decision; it does not give Git ownership of Execution's grant policy.

Preserve grammar: canonicalize declaration trim/dot/separators/consecutive whole `**`; reject absolute/`..`/backslash/NUL/leading colon/embedded `**`. Only `*` within segment and whole-segment `**` glob; all other metacharacters literal; plain entries cover subtree by full segments. Query path bytes are not trimmed; both rename ends are checked. `.fgos` is excluded even by `**`. Generated grants use the same expansion primitive. Byte paths not representable by existing string grammar are refused with lossless encoded path, never lossy-matched.

## 7. Hardened Git backend, parsers and hook contract

2026-10-19 (operation preflight): each ScopedCommit/Sync port call (including preflight, recovery, checkpoint and abort) reads effective repository config and object/ref formats once through the existing driver, validates config trust and format compatibility, and keeps those facts only on an internal handle owned by that call, seeded from the per-process stamp-validated entry for the git dir so an unchanged stamp costs no Git child. The long-lived port handle stores no config snapshot. Hook-path selection uses the same effective records. Disk topology/reciprocal registration and custody are still checked before every child. After every hook-bearing command, including rejection or transport failure, the driver invalidates the facts and reads/checks them once again before observation or cleanup (the re-read is answered from the per-process entry only while the stamp still matches, so a hook that touched config is always seen). A failed refresh fences all subsequent Git commands in that call; the next call starts fresh. Raw external Git remains outside cooperative exclusion. Publication and checkout share one ConfigStamp validation door: before each child, stat cached global/system paths, common config, worktree config and origin files; a stamp mismatch re-reads and rechecks effective config before proceeding. A change during that read refuses the operation. Post-hook refresh also compares promisor status and refuses a change, as it does for object/ref formats. Publication CAS behavior is unchanged.

One private CLI driver; no shell interpolation. Runner policy scrubs inherited `GIT_*` and constructs explicit typed per-call overrides. Preserve currently supported author/committer identity/date and trusted config-file policy; worker-controlled config env is not trusted input. Disable fsmonitor; merge rerere and autoStash off; borrowed automatic gc/maintenance off. Per-child overrides disable signature display, decoration, abbreviated commit display and color so formatted log/show/rev-list reads remain machine-readable regardless of user display configuration. Gitdir authority comes from trusted common-dir registered backlink and real path/identity, never worker `.git`. Revalidate symlink/inode substitution before effect.

**Two named env contexts, one backend owner (C6 / G-001 vs G-183):** (1) scrubbed runner calls; (2) validated hook context that retains Git-supplied index/worktree/gitdir so guards inspect exactly the commit index (including temporary index). Never scrub that authority away. Worker cannot request hook context by arbitrary env bag. Resolve hooksPath relative to trusted main; setup writes absolute owned path when filling an empty slot, preserves custom hook path and diagnoses missing enforcement.

No hardcoded trunk: explicit consumer target, remote default, then validated main attached branch; ambiguity/missing target is named. Full refs/end-of-options prevent tags/options/ranges from changing meaning. Validate commit type. Batch object/path-at-revision returns error distinct from absence.

NUL-delimited lists everywhere including worktree list; literal paths/no trim; status explicit untracked; diff `--no-relative`; scope lists `--no-renames` or return both ends. Stage literal NUL pathspec stdin, never glob argv. Stream outputs or preserve explicit equivalent ceilings (64MiB path lists, 50MiB review diff; blob guards stream); overflow is typed refusal, never silently incomplete.

Hook ordering (surviving guards; PID-grant and event-monotonicity steps **superseded**): added event-path guard (`addedEventPathRefusal`); stale-index guard; tracked `.fgos` deletion (non-event; event-file deletion only in authorized cutover grant); worker `.fgos` exclusion; root-file guard; documentation-cutover lease guard; warning-only convention; at-home check; main branch restriction; 4.3-R borrower/stranger probe; identity/beacon; beacon written last. Remove only the retired event monotonicity guard at Event Store cutover. Merge skips root allowlist exception only, not safety guards. Identity/index/blob/unknown-protocol failures close; corrupt advisory beacon warns. Physical trusted hook home and platform path equivalence are tested. Hook diagnostics retain underlying reason. Custom hook directory lacking mandatory guards is an explicit doctor gap.

**Event-file cutover grant (P-CUTOVER fold).** Today `.githooks/pre-commit` refuses every staged deletion under `.fgos/`, while Event Store §18 step 5 needs exactly one commit on main that deletes the tracked event files, `state.json` and the two backups. The grant for that one commit is a ScopedCommit whose intent carries `cutoverGrant {exactPaths, legacyRefOid, sealDigest}`, run under main-checkout 50. The hook recognizes it only when (1) it is the borrower of main 50 by the holder-lock §5 test and (2) the common-dir WAL holds a durable ScopedCommit intent of this operation whose `exactPaths` equal the staged deletions under `.fgos/` byte for byte, and whose `legacyRefOid` equals the current `refs/fgos/legacy`. No env variable, PID literal or `--no-verify` grants it. Every other `.fgos` deletion stays refused, including in that commit. The hook's support for the grant lands with the Event Store cutover change or earlier, whichever comes first, and is the same rule in both cases.

The grant also requires a durable TargetAttempt with no outcome (one shot), the SHA-256 digest of the legacy commit's `seal.json` to equal `sealDigest`, and includes the consumer-created `.fgos/events` fence when it changes. An already tracked unchanged fence is retained without forcing it into the staged delta; index-parent and repository gitdir comparisons use canonical paths.

**G-062 (owner amendment, 2026-10-20):** Git runs the commit hooks once. Preserve the original commit stderr verbatim, including whitespace and line endings; the result retains its bytes as well as its text. Failure of the commit alone does not prove pre-commit refusal. A diagnostic replay is permitted only for fgOS's owned guard: RepositoryConfig must prove the configured executable hook path's ownership/provenance. That is the identified **known safe guard invocation**; its failed replay is evidence for `hook-refused` / Unit `policy-refusal`. The diagnostic runs only through `git hook run pre-commit` in validated hook context (2), under the same custody. Its output never replaces the original commit evidence. A foreign hook is never replayed; return the generic hook-or-Git failure (`commit-rejected`, translated to Unit `git-error` / `execution-failure`) with original stderr. A post-hook config refusal also preserves that original stderr while fencing further Git calls. Git never spawns a hook file directly. `git hook run` requires the Git 2.36 release floor (E5).

2026-10-21 (phase 07 unit 25, host gate): a borrowed run (where commit hooks run) captures stderr up to 64 MiB, the ceiling holder-lock gives commit children; past it the operation is refused `borrow-output-limit: stderr`, never cut. Every other Git child keeps the 64 KiB stderr ceiling. The WAL records causes, never stderr.

### Documentation-cutover hook contract

Both hooks read the committed sidecar on `refs/heads/main`, through one shared policy helper. Pre-commit runs its lease guard before the at-home exemption, so linked worktrees are covered. While held, staged paths in the fixed scope require the committing checkout's holder token. It reads `git diff --cached --name-only -z --no-renames`; both ends of a rename count. The [lease contract](../holder-lock/spec.md#documentation-cutover-write-lease) owns commands, scope, token handling and recovery.

`.githooks/reference-transaction` has a POSIX sh front end. It buffers transaction input once and starts Node policy only for `prepared` with an exact `refs/heads/main` update. Other phases and unrelated refs exit without Node/import dependencies. Node receives the ref lines, then reads byte paths with `diff-tree -r --name-only -z --no-renames`; NUL-delimited paths never pass through shell substitution.

The reference guard admits code-only main moves while a lease is held. Scoped raw main moves are refused for everyone, holder included; deleting held main is refused too. The sole lease-transition exception requires main, first parent equal to the old OID, exactly the sidecar changed, a legal state edge, and a verified main-checkout borrower. Bootstrap/reacquire enters held from absent/released/abandoned. Leaving held preserves the holder hash: release also requires the committing checkout's token to match; owner abandon instead requires `ownerConfirmed: true` and a non-empty reason. Corrupt authority permits only that sidecar-only, direct-parent, borrowed owner-abandon repair. Borrowing main-50 alone is not holder proof.

Unreadable/corrupt authority, failed path reads, malformed NUL lists and borrower-check errors fail closed.
